High-Dimensional Robust Mean Estimation with Untrusted Batches
Maryam Aliakbarpour

We study high-dimensional mean estimation in a collaborative setting where data is contributed by
N
N
users in batches of size
n
n
. In this environment, a learner seeks to recover the mean
μ
\mu
of a true distribution
P
P
from a collection of sources that are both statistically heterogeneous and potentially malicious. We formalize this challenge through a
double corruption
landscape: an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users are entirely adversarial, while the remaining “good” users provide data from distributions that are related to
P
P
, but deviate by a proximity parameter
α
\alpha
.
Unlike existing work on the untrusted batch model, which typically measures this deviation via total variation distance in discrete settings, we address the continuous, high-dimensional regime under two natural variants for deviation: (1) good batches are drawn from distributions with a mean-shift of
α
\sqrt{\alpha}
, or (2) an
α
\alpha
-fraction of samples within each good batch are adversarially corrupted. In particular, the second model presents significant new challenges: in high dimensions, unlike discrete settings, even a small fraction of sample-level corruption can shift empirical means and covariances arbitrarily.
We provide two Sum-of-Squares (SoS) based algorithms to navigate this tiered corruption. Our algorithms achieve the minimax-optimal error rate
O
⁡
(
ε
/
n
+
d
/
n
​
N
+
α
)
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon/n}+\sqrt{d/nN}+\sqrt{\alpha})
, demonstrating that while heterogeneity
α
\alpha
represents an inherent statistical difficulty, the influence of adversarial users is suppressed by a factor of
1
/
n
1/\sqrt{n}
due to the internal averaging afforded by the batch structure.

Introduction
In the modern data ecosystem, information is increasingly aggregated from decentralized and heterogeneous sources
[
41
,
27
,
42
,
33
]
. Because these data are generated across diverse institutions and devices (subject to shifting demographics, varying measurement procedures, and fluctuating contexts) the classical i.i.d. assumption is no longer a valid assumption
[
15
,
38
,
39
]
. While pooling such information allows entities to solve problems that are
statistically impossible
in isolation, it requires a principled framework that can distinguish between two distinct forms of data deviation: natural statistical heterogeneity among legitimate participants and strategic interference from adversarial ones.
We address these challenges through the lens of the
untrusted batch model
[
36
]
, providing a rigorous foundation for reliable inference by introducing a
double corruption
landscape. This landscape captures the tiered complexity of real-world data: we consider a learner seeking to estimate the
mean
μ
\mu
of a target distribution
P
P
over
ℝ
d
\mathbb{R}^{d}
using data from
N
N
users, where each user contributes a
batch
of
n
n
samples. In this setting, an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users are
adversarially corrupted
, while the remaining “good” users provide data from distributions that are related to
P
P
but exhibit diminishing quality, governed by a proximity parameter
α
\alpha
. We consider two natural variants for this relationship: either the good users’ distributions have a mean shifted by
α
\sqrt{\alpha}
from the truth, or an
α
\alpha
-fraction of the samples
within
each good batch are themselves adversarially corrupted.
This second variant marks a significant departure from existing results in the untrusted batch model. While prior research
[
6
,
24
]
has primarily characterized the quality of good batches through total variation (TV) distance, such measures typically account for randomized replacement of
α
\alpha
fraction of the data. In the continuous high-dimensional regime, this distinction is critical: unlike TV-bounded shifts, an adversary can inspect the samples within a batch and strategically replace an
α
\alpha
-fraction of them with new points. This allows the adversary to erase legitimate signals and induce shifts in the empirical mean and covariance by an arbitrary distance—an impact far more pronounced than the randomized replacements captured by distributional proximity.
By providing algorithms that navigate this dual-layer of adversarial corruption and mean-shifts, we provide the minimax-optimal error rate
O
⁡
(
ε
/
n
+
d
/
n
​
N
+
α
)
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon/n}+\sqrt{d/nN}+\sqrt{\alpha})
. This rate reveals a fundamental insight: while the heterogeneity
α
\alpha
represents an inherent statistical difficulty, the adversary’s influence on fraction of “bad” users is suppressed by a factor of
1
/
n
1/\sqrt{n}
. This occurs because the
n
n
samples within a batch provide a more stable statistical signature than individual points, allowing the learner to partially “average away” the effect of user-level corruption as the batch size increases.
1.1
Problem Setup
We study the
untrusted batch model
introduced in
[
36
]
. We consider an unknown distribution
P
P
on
ℝ
d
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean
μ
\mu
and bounded covariance
Σ
⪯
𝕀
d
\Sigma\preceq\mathbb{I}_{d}
. Both
μ
\mu
and
Σ
\Sigma
are unknown to the algorithm. The goal is to estimate the mean by leveraging data contributed by
N
N
other users (or collaborators) in
ℓ
2
\ell_{2}
norm. Each user
i
∈
[
N
]
i\in[N]
possesses a local dataset consisting of
n
n
data points, referred to as a
batch
. The setting is particularly interesting in the case where
n
≪
d
n\ll d
, where the data of each user is not sufficient to perform mean estimation on their own, but can be jointly leveraged to recover the mean accurately.
The users in our model are not homogeneous: some may provide statistically relevant data, while others may be unreliable or even adversarial. Our framework explicitly models both cases as
good
and
bad
users. We assume that at most an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of the users are
bad
. Formally, there exists a subset
ℬ
⊆
[
N
]
\mathcal{B}\subseteq[N]
of size at most
ε
​
N
\varepsilon N
such that for every
i
∈
ℬ
i\in\mathcal{B}
, the dataset of user
i
i
may be chosen arbitrarily by an adversary, with no assumptions on its distribution or quality. The remaining users,
𝒢
=
[
N
]
∖
ℬ
\mathcal{G}=[N]\setminus\mathcal{B}
, are
good users
. The informativeness of their data is governed by a parameter
α
\alpha
, which measures how statistically related their data is to the target user’s distribution. We consider two natural variants that capture distinct notions of relevance.
Mean Shift Model.
Figure 1
:
Mean Shift Model. Illustration of Problem
1.1
: each good user provides samples drawn from a distribution whose mean lies within a
α
\sqrt{\alpha}
-neighborhood of the target mean
μ
\mu
, while an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users (yellow) are fully adversarial and may provide arbitrary samples.
In the first variant, as a motivating example, we quantify heterogeneity as a mean-shift, where the distribution of each good user is centered within an
α
\sqrt{\alpha}
-neighborhood of the target mean
μ
\mu
. Our problem setup is formally defined as follows:
Problem 1.1
(Mean shift)
.
Consider parameters
α
,
ε
∈
(
0
,
1
)
\alpha,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, a target mean
μ
∈
ℝ
d
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
N
N
users, each providing a batch of
n
n
data points in
ℝ
d
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We are guaranteed that at least a
(
1
−
ε
)
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of these users are good; each good user
i
∈
𝒢
i\in\mathcal{G}
provides
n
n
i.i.d. samples drawn from an unknown distribution
P
i
P_{i}
with mean
μ
i
\mu_{i}
and covariance
Σ
i
⪯
𝕀
d
\Sigma_{i}\preceq\mathbb{I}_{d}
. The relevance of a good user’s data is quantified by its proximity to the target:
‖
μ
i
−
μ
‖
2
≤
α
\|\mu_{i}-\mu\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\alpha}
. The remaining
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users are adversarial and may contribute entirely arbitrary batches. Our goal is to robustly estimate the true mean
μ
\mu
in
ℓ
2
\ell_{2}
distance.
Adversarial Model.
Figure 2
:
Adversarial Model. Illustration of Problem
1.2
: beyond an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of entirely adversarial users (yellow clusters), each good user’s batch contains an
α
\alpha
-fraction of adversarially corrupted samples (yellow points within blue clusters), resulting in a two-level corruption model.
In the second variant, we consider a more malicious form of heterogeneity where even the good users’ datasets contain corrupted samples. This introduces a
double-layered contamination
: beyond the
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of entirely adversarial users, an
α
\alpha
-fraction of samples within each good batch is also replaced by corrupted points.
Problem 1.2
(Adversarial)
.
Consider parameters
α
,
ε
∈
(
0
,
1
)
\alpha,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, a target distribution
P
P
over
ℝ
d
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with mean
μ
\mu
and covariance
Σ
⪯
𝕀
d
\Sigma\preceq\mathbb{I}_{d}
, and
N
N
users, each providing a batch of
n
n
data points in
ℝ
d
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We are guaranteed that at least a
(
1
−
ε
)
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of these users are good; each good user
i
∈
𝒢
i\in\mathcal{G}
draws
n
n
i.i.d. samples from
P
P
. Then an
α
\alpha
-fraction of the
n
n
samples are replaced by arbitrary, adversarially chosen points. This is in addition to the
entirely adversarial datasets of the remaining
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users. Our goal is to robustly estimate the true mean
μ
\mu
in
ℓ
2
\ell_{2}
distance.
This framework captures a broad range of realistic challenges: some users may be entirely unreliable, while even honest users may possess partially compromised datasets due to sensor errors, distribution shifts, or localized interference.
Corruption Model.
In both variants, we adopt the
strong contamination model
(see Definition
1.1
). Conceptually, the data of all users are first generated according to their ideal distributions, after which a
globally coordinated adversary
is allowed to inspect the entire dataset of batches and modify the samples subject to its corruption budgets. This ensures that the corruption is not merely independent across users, but can be strategically orchestrated to maximize the bias of the global estimate based on the specific realization of all uncorrupted data.
Definition 1.3
(Strong contamination model
[
14
]
)
.
Given
ε
∈
(
0
,
1
)
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and a distribution
𝒟
\mathcal{D}
,
ε
\varepsilon
-strong contamination model works as follows:
n
n
samples are drawn i.i.d. from
𝒟
\mathcal{D}
. An adversary receives all samples and can change up to
ε
​
n
\varepsilon n
of them arbitrarily. The algorithm takes the modified samples as input.
1.2
Related Work
Robust statistics
Robustly learning an underlying parameter from an unknown distribution is a long-standing field in statistics, dating back to
[
21
]
. The Tukey median
[
40
]
achieves the optimal error for mean estimation but is inefficient in high-dimensions. The works
[
10
,
31
]
resolved the computation inefficiency and inspired many subsequent works to design efficient and robust learning algorithms in various settings of practical interest.
[
7
,
12
]
further improve the computational efficiency to near-linear time.
[
5
]
studied the heterogeneous corruption rates for each sample.
[
11
]
studied sparse mean estimation.
[
8
]
studied high-dimensional robust mean estimation using gradient descent.
The sum-of-squares method
[
34
,
32
]
has proven to be a powerful tool for robust statistics
[
29
,
19
,
28
]
. It provides a unified framework to design efficient algorithms as opposed to relying on problem-specific heuristics. For many robust estimation problems, optimal or best-known time and sample complexity can often be achieved using the sum-of-squares approach
[
2
,
30
,
17
,
20
,
9
]
.
Robust estimation from corrupted batches
[
36
]
initiated the study of robust density estimation from corrupted batches for discrete distributions under the TV distance. Subsequent works
[
6
,
24
]
further improved the computational and sample complexities. All of these prior works
[
36
,
6
,
24
]
typically assume that the data from each good user follow a mildly perturbed distribution—for example, each user’s samples are drawn from some distribution
P
i
P_{i}
satisfying
TV
⁡
(
P
i
,
P
)
≤
Θ
⁡
(
α
)
\mathrm{TV}(P_{i},P)\leq\Theta(\alpha)
.
In contrast, our work is the first to address high-dimensional mean estimation under a fully general, multi-level corruption model. We introduce and analyze a setting in which corruption can occur at both the user level and the sample level: beyond corrupting entire users, an adversary may arbitrarily corrupt up to an
α
\alpha
-fraction of samples within each good user. This strictly generalizes the settings considered in previous works.
This generalization is particularly challenging in the continuous, high-dimensional regime. In discrete distributions, corrupting an
α
\alpha
-fraction of samples in a good batch leads to at most an
α
\alpha
increase in the
ℓ
1
\ell_{1}
distance of the empirical histogram. In contrast, for high-dimensional distributions, even a small
α
\alpha
-fraction of corrupted samples can arbitrarily shift the empirical mean of a batch.
In addition to density estimation of discrete distributions,
[
23
,
25
]
studied robust density estimation of structured continuous distribution as well as
𝒜
k
\mathcal{A}_{k}
distance, which is always upper bounded by TV distance.
[
23
]
also designed algorithms for piecewise interval classification.
[
26
]
analyzed linear regression with the presence of heterogeneous data batches.
Other related robust learning models
[
1
]
studied robust personalized federated learning under Byzantine attack, where each user has clean local data from some distribution and a personalized optimization objective, but a fraction of users may be untruthful.
[
35
]
studied robust distribution learning under Wasserstein distance where an adversary may arbitrarily corrupt a fraction of samples and locally perturb the remainder with bounded average magnitude. Our adversarial corruption model (Problem
1.2
), the main focus of our work, is stronger than both works in that our good users may suffer from adversarial corruption that could shift the local mean arbitrarily.
Organization
The remaining parts of this paper are organized as follows. In
Section
2
, we present the main results of our work. In
Section
3
, we present the preliminaries and notation. In
Section
4
, we provide a detailed overview of the techniques used to obtain our main results.
2
Main Results
We first review standard results in high-dimensional statistics. In the standard setting without any corruption, estimating the mean of a
d
d
-dimensional distribution with bounded covariance from
n
n
samples yields a minimax error bound of
Θ
⁡
(
d
/
n
)
\Theta(\sqrt{d/n})
in
ℓ
2
\ell_{2}
norm.
When an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of the samples may be adversarially corrupted, there exists a polynomial-time algorithm
[
10
,
31
]
that achieves an error of
O
⁡
(
ε
)
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
using
n
=
O
~
​
(
d
/
ε
)
n=\widetilde{O}\left(d/\varepsilon\right)
samples.
We then discuss simple solutions to some special cases of our problem. When
α
=
0
\alpha=0
(for both corruption models), we can simply run the standard robust mean estimation algorithm
[
10
]
on the empirical mean of each user with corruption parameter

\varepsilon

1
/

1/n
, we can achieve an error of

)

Ω

(

.
On the other hand, when

\varepsilon=0

as long as

The main question is what happens when both

>

\alpha,\varepsilon>0

+

\sqrt{\varepsilon/n}+\sqrt{\alpha}

First, we state the result for mean shift corruption:

(Mean shift)
.

Remark 2.3
.
In Problem
1.2
when
α
>
0
\alpha>0
, at least one sample from each user is corrupted, so we implicitly require

≥
1
/
n
\alpha\geq 1/n
. Thus,
α
≥
ε
n
\sqrt{\alpha}\geq\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{n}}
, and we do not have the extra
ε

term in the error.
Unknown corruption
In practice,
ε
,
α
\varepsilon,\alpha
may not be known in advance. We can adapt to the unknown corruption level by performing binary search over the parameters, and stop when we reach beyond the “true” corruption level, leading to only an extra logarithmic factor in the sample complexity and running time. The stopping criteria could be determined using intersection of confidence balls
[
22
]
or tolerant testing
[
13
,
4
]
(which requires
O
⁡
(
d
)
O(\sqrt{d})
clean samples). We will elaborate on this in
Appendix
E
.
3
Preliminaries
In this section, we first introduce all notation used throughout the paper. Then, in
Section
3.1
, we give the formal definition of the sum-of-squares proof; in

3.2
Pseudo-distributions
Pseudo-distribution is a generalization of probability distributions in that their “probability” weight can be negative and “expectations” are only guaranteed to be non-negative for square polynomials of finite degree.

This relaxation is crucial for the Sum-of-Squares framework: rather than searching over discrete or combinatorial objects directly, we optimize over pseudo-expectations that behave like expectations on all low-degree polynomials. In particular, any feasible SoS solution corresponds to a pseudo-distribution whose pseudo-expectation operator satisfies basic probabilistic inequalities (such as Cauchy–Schwarz) up to the prescribed degree.

4
Technical Overview
In
Section

, we present a brief overview of SoS for robust mean estimation. In
Section

, we introduce how to adapt traditional SoS to mean shift corruption (Problem

). In

, we address the more challenging Problem
1.2
by designing a novel two-level refinement polynomial system and provide the proof overview.
4.1
Brief Review of SoS for Robust Mean Estimation
The key structural insight for robust mean estimation is the following: if one can identify a subset of samples
S
S
that contains at least a
(
1
−
ε
)
(1-\varepsilon)
fraction of the data and whose empirical covariance is small, then the empirical mean of
S
S
must be close to the true mean. Intuitively, low empirical covariance ensures that no small group of samples can “pull” the mean in any particular direction. Thus, even if
S
S
includes a small number of corrupted points, their collective influence on the mean is limited.
The main algorithmic challenge is to efficiently find such a subset
S
S
. A powerful framework for this task is provided by the
sum-of-squares (SoS)
[
34
,
29
]
relaxation. In this approach, the search for a large, low-covariance subset is formulated as a system of polynomial constraints over indicator variables representing sample membership to
S
S
. Formally, the polynomial constraints should at least include:
1.
S
S
is sufficiently large:
with

∈
{

,
1
}

denotes whether or not the

∑

=
1

in
S

(
1
−
ε
)
​
n
(1-\varepsilon)n
, and
2.
Bounded covariance:
We use
Z

to denote the selected data point, which should satisfy
1

1

(

The SoS hierarchy then yields a tractable convex relaxation of this polynomial system, from which one can extract an accurate mean estimate. The standard SoS analysis involves two main parts:
Satisfiability:
With high probability, a feasible solution to the SoS relaxation exists when the data satisfy the assumed corruption model.
A natural idea is to prove that the set of clean samples satisfies the constraints with high probability. However, for bounded covariance random variables, we cannot directly obtain a high-probability bound on
1

(

). Samples from this truncated distribution satisfies bounded empirical covariance with high probability when
n

Identifiability:
Any feasible (or approximately feasible) SoS solution corresponds to a mean estimate that is provably close to the true mean of the uncorrupted distribution.
For a review of the SoS preliminaries, see
Section
3.1
.
4.2

Algorithm ideas.
In this model, each good user’s data come from a neighboring distribution and contain no additional corruption, so we can view each user’s empirical mean as a
low-variance point
whose expectation is slightly shifted from the target mean. Bad users, on the other hand, may contribute arbitrary empirical means. Therefore, we denote all the

Ω
⁡
(
ε
/
n
)
\Omega\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon/n}\right)
on the estimation error.
Finally, combining with the lower bound in the classical high-dimensional mean estimation
Θ
⁡
(
d

​
n
)
\Theta\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{Nn}}\right)
, we have shown that our result is
minimax-optimal
.
For more information, we kindly refer the readers to
Appendix

.
4.3
Handling Small Corruption in Good Users (Problem
1.2
).
In this setting, the bad samples of good users can shift the local mean arbitrarily. Thus, the per-user empirical mean is no longer a low-variance quantity, which means the solution in
Section
4.2
cannot be applied, calling for a new colution
Simple solutions
As discussed in
Section
2
, a naive solution is to leverage the observation that the total fraction of corrupted samples is
ε
+
α
\varepsilon+\alpha
, so running the standard robust mean estimation gives an error of
O
⁡
(
ε
+
α
+
d
N
​

)

.
Another naive solution is to apply robust mean estimation for each user individually. However, achieving an accuracy of

=

⁡
(

/

)

samples. It leaves the question whether

=

⁡
(

)

.
Algorithm ideas.
We design a novel SoS formulation
𝖡
\mathsf{B}
formally defined in Definition

, which synergizes the sample constraint
Section
4.1
and the user-level constraint in
Section
4.2
,
1.
Crude refinement:
The purpose is to filter out gross outliers among bad users and corrupted samples from good users. These constraints are similar to the standard SoS constraints over all samples, with the key distinction that we additionally add an indicator

}

=

. All the selected samples

(

2
​

)

.
2.
User-level refinement:
The second set of constraints performs an additional selection step for good users, which leverages the concentration of good users and enforces that the empirical covariance of their (cleaned) empirical means remains small, similar to

. More concretely, this user-level filtering requires the number of users

By coupling these two levels of constraints, our SoS algorithm enjoys the benefits of both the large sample size of all users as well as concentration within each user, thereby achieving optimal error while remaining computationally and sample efficient.

Appendix
Roadmap
In
Appendix
A
, we present the mathematical background of SoS and basic probabilistic tools.
In
Appendix
B
, we introduce the SoS algorithm for the traditional robust mean estimation problem, where the corruption level
ε
∈
(
0
,
1
)
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
is given and
α
=
0
\alpha=0
.
In
Appendix
C
, we analyze our first problem (
Problem
1.1
), present our main upper bound result (
Theorem
C.1
), and provide its complete proof. In
Appendix
D
, we analyze our second problem (
Problem
1.2
), present our main upper bound result (
Theorem
D.1
), and provide its complete proof.
In
Appendix
E
, we further apply this technique to the settings of
Problem
1.1
and
Problem
1.2
, thereby enabling our algorithms for these more complex problems to handle unknown corruption levels
ε
\varepsilon
and
α
\alpha
.
Finally, in
Appendix
F
, we present our lower bound results for
Problem
1.1
and
Problem
1.2
, showing that our upper bounds are minimax optimal.

Appendix B
Robust Mean Estimation Via SoS
In
Section
B.1
, we extend the traditional SoS approach on arbitrary bound on the empirical covariance. In
Section
B.2
, we improve the result of
[
14
, Proposition 3.9]
showing that using truncation is sufficient to obtain a stable subset with high probability.
B.1
Accurate Estimation of the Empirical Mean
Any large subset of points with a sufficiently low empirical covariance will have an empirical mean close to the true mean
μ
\mu
. We know that the set of all clean samples satisfies this property with high probability. The crucial implication, however, is that a valid subset
S
S
does not need to be perfectly clean. It can include corrupted points, provided they are not
harmful
in a way that significantly pulls the mean and consequently increases the set’s empirical covariance. This gives us a
verifiable criterion
: instead of needing to know which samples are clean, we only need to find a large subset that satisfies this geometric condition.
To identify such a large subset, we employ the SoS framework from
[
37
,
18
]
. This framework reformulates algorithmic and statistical problems into an algebraic representation described by a collection of polynomial constraints.
In the context of robust mean estimation, these polynomial constraints can be formulated as:
W
i
2
\displaystyle W_{i}^{2}
=
W
i
\displaystyle=W_{i}
(6)
∑
i
=
1
n
W
i
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}W_{i}
=
(
1
−
ε
)
​
n
\displaystyle=(1-\varepsilon)n
(7)
W
i
​
(
Z
i
−
v
i
)
\displaystyle W_{i}(Z_{i}-v_{i})
=
0
\displaystyle=0
(8)
Z
¯
\displaystyle\overline{Z}
=
1
n
​
∑
i
=
1
n
Z
i
\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
(9)
1
n
​
∑

=
1

(

)

(

−

Appendix C
Learning From Corrupted Batches With Bounded User Means
In this section, our goal is to analyze Problem

, where an

Specifically, in

, we restate the problem setting of Problem

and present our upper-bound result. In
Section

, we formally define the polynomial system
𝖠
\mathsf{A}
and describe our SoS-based algorithmic framework for achieving this upper bound; we also provide an informal proof sketch explaining why this design attains the optimal error rate. In

Section

, we give a formal proof of correctness and derive the sample-complexity guarantee for our main result.
C.1

Now, we restate the formal problem setup for learning from corrupted batches with bounded user means.

(Matrix Chernoff Inequality, Theorem 3.12 from

C.4
Identifiability
After showing the satisfiability our polynomial system

(Definition

), we present how we can use the polynomial system

In this section, we establish the identifiability of the true mean under the polynomial system

\mathsf{A}
.
After proving in

that the system is satisfiable with high probability, we now show that any feasible
pseudoexpectation satisfying the same constraints must correspond to an accurate estimate of the true mean
μ

.
The argument proceeds in two steps.
First, leveraging the bounded-covariance condition enforced by the SoS constraints,
we show that the pseudoexpectation output

Appendix D
Learning From Corrupted Batches With Fractional User Data Corruption
In this section, our goal is to analyze Problem
1.2
, where an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users may be adversarially strong contaminated and may
provide adversarially or arbitrarily chosen samples, and for all the
(
1
−
ε
)
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of uncorrupted users, their
α
\alpha
-fraction of samples may be adversarially or arbitrarily contaminated. This hierarchical structure—with inter-user corruption parameter
ε
\varepsilon
and intra-user corruption
parameter
α
\alpha
—makes the setting substantially more challenging and necessitates a refined SoS
formulation that simultaneously enforces consistency across both user and sample levels.
Specifically, in
Section
D.1
, we restate the problem setting of Problem
1.2
and present our upper-bound result. In
Section
D.2
, we formally define the polynomial system
𝖡
\mathsf{B}
and describe our SoS-based algorithmic framework for achieving this upper bound; we also provide an informal proof sketch explaining why this design attains the optimal error rate. In
Section
D.3
, we establish the satisfiability of the polynomial system, namely the existence of a feasible solution with high probability. Finally, in
Section
D.4
, we give a formal proof of correctness and derive the sample-complexity guarantee for our main result.
D.1
Main Result
Now, we restate our formal problem setup and its corresponding main result.
See
1.2
A straightforward approach to the problem is to ignore the two-level structure of corruption
and simply treat all the samples across all users as being jointly
(
ε
+
α
)
(\varepsilon+\alpha)
-corrupted.
Under this view, one can directly apply a standard robust mean estimation algorithm designed
for an
(
ε
+
α
)
(\varepsilon+\alpha)
-fraction of arbitrary corruptions, yielding an estimator with
accuracy of
O
⁡
(
ε
+
α
)
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon+\alpha})
.
However, this approach is suboptimal because it fails to exploit the fact that
most users are only
partially
corrupted—each good user has at most an
α
\alpha
-fraction
of bad samples, while only an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users are entirely unreliable.
By explicitly modeling this hierarchical corruption structure in our SoS formulation,
we are able to isolate the good users and leverage the concentration within their data,
thereby improving the estimation error bound beyond
O
⁡
(
ε
+
α
)
O(\sqrt{\varepsilon+\alpha})
.

D.2
Algorithmic Design and Proof Overview
We give a detailed proof for the satisfiability (Lemma
D.2
) and identifiability (Lemma
D.2
) in
Section
D.3
and
Section
D.4
. The goal of this section is to state these lemmas, present a proof overview, and explain how we can design our algorithm (Algorithm
2
) stated in the theorem.
Algorithmic design.
Our polynomial time SoS algorithm (Algorithm
2
) is based on the polynomial system
𝖡
\mathsf{B}
(defined below in Definition
D.2
). Since each user’s dataset may itself contain corrupted samples, our algorithm must jointly
handle both user-level and sample-level contamination. We therefore extend the SoS framework by introducing a two-layer polynomial system
𝖡
\mathsf{B}
that captures this hierarchical corruption
structure. At the sample level, the system enforces that, within each user, at least a
(
1
−
α
)
(1-\alpha)
-fraction of samples are consistent and their empirical covariance remains bounded, thereby filtering out local outliers. At the user level, it enforces that at least a
(
1
−
ε
)
(1-\varepsilon)
-fraction of users are globally consistent and that the empirical covariance of their aggregated (cleaned) means is spectrally bounded.
Together, these constraints enable the algorithm to recover the true mean
μ
\mu
with improved precision,
leveraging the fact that the clean samples within each user further reduce variance by a factor of
1
/
n
1/n
.

D.4
Identifiability
The goal of this section is to establish the identifiability of the true mean
μ
\mu
under the two-level corruption model captured by the polynomial system
𝖡
\mathsf{B}
.
After showing in
Section
D.3
that the system is satisfiable with high probability,
we now argue that any feasible pseudoexpectation consistent with the SoS constraints
must correspond to an accurate estimate of
μ
\mu
.
At a high level, our proof proceeds in two stages: First each good user may contain at most an
α
\alpha
-fraction of corrupted samples. The SoS constraints ensure that the empirical mean of the remaining
(
1
−
α
)
​
n
(1-\alpha)n
samples has covariance bounded by
1
n
​
𝕀
d
\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{I}_{d}
. Hence, the user-level cleaned means
{
Y
i
}
\{Y_{i}\}
concentrate tightly around
their expectations.
Second, an
ε
\varepsilon
-fraction of users may be entirely corrupted,
but the remaining users have bounded deviations.
Using the bounded-covariance conditions enforced by the SoS system,
we show that the pseudoexpectation output
𝔼
~
​
[
Y
¯
]
\widetilde{\E}[\overline{Y}]
(and thus the estimator
μ
^
\widehat{\mu}
) cannot deviate far from the mean
of the clean users.
The total deviation accumulates additively from the two levels of corruption:
ε
n
\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{n}}
from user-level corruption
and
α
\sqrt{\alpha}
from the corruption of samples within each uncorrupted user.
Together, these steps show that any feasible pseudoexpectation satisfying
the SoS constraints must yield a mean estimate
μ
^
\widehat{\mu}
satisfying
‖
μ
^
−
μ
‖
2
<
O
⁡
(
ε
n
+
α
)
\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\mu}-\mu\right\|_{2}<O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon}{n}}+\sqrt{\alpha}\right)
thereby establishing identifiability of
μ
\mu
under the hierarchical corruption model.

Even a small number of clean samples from a user can be leveraged to
verify
the accuracy of the estimated mean. This verification step enables the user to
adaptively adjust
to the effective level of corruption relevant to their own distribution—allowing for personalized robustness that naturally reflects user-specific data quality.
Although
[
22
]
also removed the need to know the corruption level by combining estimates obtained at multiple guessed parameters, it inherently yields only a constant-factor approximation and incurs repeated estimator runs. Tolerant testing instead offers an absolute certification of accuracy using a small number of clean samples, allowing the algorithm to adaptively stop at the optimal rate for the true corruption level and achieve minimax-optimal error without post-hoc aggregation. It can further returns the unknown

F.3
Permutation Invariant
Under our two-level corruption model, an adversary first selects a subset of
ε
​
N
\varepsilon N
users to corrupt and, for each uncorrupted user, a subset of
α
​
n
\alpha n
samples to corrupt. A priori, the adversary may choose these
subsets in an arbitrary way, leading to a
complicated pattern of corrupted entries in the
(
i
,
j
)
(i,j)
-th entry
X
i
,
j
X_{i,j}
.
However, if the estimator is permutation invariant, then only the counts of corrupted users and corrupted samples matter, not their specific indices.
Consequently, we may equivalently consider a symmetrized case in which
the adversary first fixes an arbitrary corruption pattern and we then apply a
uniform random permutation
π
\pi
to the users and independent uniform random
permutations
{
σ
i
}
\{\sigma_{i}\}
to the samples within each user. From the point of
view of a permutation-invariant estimator, this symmetrized case is
identical to the original one.
In particular, after this random symmetrization, the distribution of each entry
X
i
,
j
X_{i,j}
is the same across all
(
i
,
j
)
(i,j)
:
X
i
,
j
∼
{
P
0
,
with probability
​
1
−
ρ
,
Q
,
with probability
​
ρ
,
X_{i,j}\sim\begin{cases}P_{0},&\text{with probability }1-\rho,\\[5.69054pt]
Q,&\text{with probability }\rho,\end{cases}
for some contamination rate
ρ
\rho
that depends only on
(
ε
,
α
)
(\varepsilon,\alpha)
and on whether we are in the user-level or sample-level corruption regime.
Under the hypothesis
H
0
H_{0}
there is no corruption, so
X
i
,
j
∼
P
0
X_{i,j}\sim P_{0}
for all
(
i
,
j
)
(i,j)
; under the alternative
H
1
H_{1}
, each
X
i
,
j
X_{i,j}
follows the above
(
1
−
ρ
)
​
P
0
+
ρ
​
Q
(1-\rho)P_{0}+\rho Q
mixture. Thus, the
complicated adversarial pattern of corruptions in the original matrix model is,
after symmetrization, equivalent to an i.i.d. product model over entries.

Instructions for reporting errors
We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below:
Click the "Report Issue"
(
)
button, located in the page header.
Tip:
You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
Our team has already identified
the following issues
. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all.
Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a
list of packages that need conversion
, and welcome
developer contributions
.
